# Changelog

**English** | [日本語](CHANGELOG.ja.md)

All notable user‑facing changes to RoamSwitch. The Mac
edition (1.x) and the Linux edition (1.x, a separate series) are versioned
independently. Older releases are summarized in ranges.

---

## AccessScope

A security diagnosis tool for access logs. It is a separate app (Apache-2.0): the Server Edition .deb / .rpm packages carry only the command (`accessscope`; from 1.11.2 they also carry the PersonalSOC and PathScope commands); the Linux client edition and the Mac app carry the command and a window app.

### 1.0.9

Adds a decoy page aimed at AI agents and a ledger of detections that could not be evaluated for the log format.

- **Added: detections that could not be evaluated because of the log format go into a ledger (`not_evaluable`).** False positives on formats without a Referer (common-format logs) are fixed. `--fail-on-partial` (exit code 4) does not count `not_evaluable`.
- **Added: sub-second rhythm, measured from the fractional seconds in the log, as a supporting signal for "machine".** It is not added up with the other signals.
- **Added (low confidence): a chain of API discovery and ID scanning after several account sign-ups (`web_signup_chain`), and a run of 404s on random-looking one-segment paths after a favicon fetch (`web_favicon_chain`), reported as low-confidence hypotheses.**
- **Added (experimental, off by default): a decoy page aimed at AI agents, as the fourth signal of the AI-client verdict.**
- **Added: the CLI output can be filtered with closed fields, no regular expressions** (`--filter`, `--filter-or`, `--min-severity`, `--limit`).
- Verification: 665 workspace tests pass (without the GUI crate; Docker; 0 failures). Whether a real AI agent follows the decoy page has not been checked.

### 1.0.8

- **Added: sources that came from AI crawlers or from LLM SDKs and agent tools are shown with three separate kinds of evidence (AI client attribution).** The evidence is (1) a user agent that names an AI company (OpenAI, Anthropic, Perplexity, Google's Vertex AI crawler) together with that company's published IP ranges, (2) the rhythm of request intervals (20 or more intervals from the same source, a median of 2 seconds or more, and a coefficient of variation of 0.35 or less or a most common interval (±1 second) of 60% or more), and (3) LLM SDK names (`OpenAI/Python` and so on) and the headers SDKs add (`x-stainless-*` and so on). The three are listed side by side, not added up, and the confidence is the number of kinds of evidence (one is tentative, two likely, three strong). The result appears in the summary line, the tables (default output, `--plain`, `--report`), the app's dashboard and `web.ai_attribution` in the JSON. It is not part of the findings or the risk score. No network and no LLM are used.
  - A source with only a regular rhythm is not listed (monitoring and feed readers are regular too).
  - A source that names an AI company from outside its published ranges is shown as a possible fake crawler (`web_fake_crawler`) and is not counted as evidence. An IP taken from `X-Forwarded-For` is not matched against the ranges.
  - SDK headers are read only from logs that record headers by name (Caddy's JSON, Traefik set to log headers). Headers that an nginx `escape=json` log records in fields such as `$http_x_stainless_lang` are not read; the report then says that SDK headers were not checked.
- **Fixed: in nginx JSON logs, the `$request_*` fields were treated as named headers.**
- **Fixed: the evidence of an attack chain showed the same line twice.**
- Measured: of the web cases in the evaluation corpus, the 1,338 that contain no AI company or SDK marker and no address in an AI company's published ranges produced 0 wrong entries (our own test data). Listing AI crawlers and SDKs correctly is checked with synthetic logs (GPTBot, SDK user agents, `x-stainless-*` headers, impersonation, `X-Forwarded-For`). It has not been measured on real server logs.
- Checks: the 679 workspace tests pass, including 466 in the core lib, 31 in the CLI and 44 in the app (Mac; 0 failed; the 3 ignored were already there).

### 1.0.7

- **Fixed: reports in English and other languages contained the Japanese middle dot and wave dash.** "2 kinds of logs (Web・SSH)" now reads "(Web, SSH)", and the "〜" in the log period becomes "–" (in English and others), each in the way of its language. The period field in the app does the same. Japanese is unchanged.
- Checks: the 661 workspace tests pass, including 451 in the core lib, 30 in the CLI and 43 in the app (Mac; 0 failed; the 3 ignored were already there).

### 1.0.6

- **Fixed: `accessscope … | head` and `accessscope-eval … | head` printed a panic message.** They now exit with code 141 without it.
- **Fixed: the reason a file could not be read stayed in English inside a Japanese report.** It is shown in the display language (for example "cannot read journald: run as root or as a user in the adm / systemd-journal group", "failed to read (OS error 5)"). `code` is added to the JSON sources.
- **Fixed: a broken state file or `discovered.json` was reported as "no earlier record" and overwritten.** It is now reported as "cannot be confirmed", and an unusable file is kept as `<name>.corrupt-…`, the newest 3 (a file of another version is kept too). A file given with `--state` is handled the same way.
- **Changed: the material given to the LLM is in English** (`--show-prompt`, the preview_prompt in the app). The report is written in the display language as before, and the `--llm` draft moves from fixed Japanese to the display language. Secrets are masked as `[REDACTED]` in every language (`[email]` for e-mail addresses).
- Checks: the tests pass, including 450 in the core lib, 30 in the CLI and 43 in the app (Mac; the 2 ignored in the app were already there).

### 1.0.5

- **Changed: English output picks the singular or plural to match the number** (German, Spanish, French, Italian and Portuguese too).
- **Changed: reports, blocklists and RoamSwitch config drafts are written readable by the owner only (0600) when the file is newly created.**
- **Fixed: an IP-based suppression rule could make a high-severity chain disappear without going through the decision.**

### 1.0.4

- **Added: ClaudeBot, Claude-User and Claude-SearchBot of Anthropic are checked against the list of IPs that Anthropic publishes (14 entries).** A request from inside the official ranges counts as a "verified crawler" and is left out of the reconnaissance and similar findings; one that claims the name from outside the ranges is reported as "suspected impersonation of a known crawler". Before, Anthropic was treated as having no published list, so nothing was verified or flagged. Meta, Yandex and Baidu have no machine-readable official list and are still not judged.
- **Updated: the snapshot of the crawler IP ranges was taken again on 2026-10-29** (the lists of Google, OpenAI and Perplexity had changed).
- Checks: the workspace tests pass, including new tests (a ClaudeBot inside the official range is left out, and one claiming the name from outside is reported as impersonation).

### 0.1.0 - 1.0.3 (summarized)

The contents of these releases, grouped by theme. The full text of each entry is in this file's git history.

- (1.0.3) **Added: detections for web reconnaissance and tunnelling.** Hunting for secret and configuration files (`web_asset_harvest`), API probing (`web_api_probe`), parameter fuzzing (`web_param_fuzz`), site walking (`web_probe_walk`), and a suspected HTTP tunnel (`web_tunnel_suspect`). The known gaps missed in the evaluation data went from 14 to 6. A tunnel cannot be stated as fact because the log has no body, so it is shown as a suspicion.
- (1.0.3) **Added: host records are read to raise the confidence of a web attack** (RoamSwitch execution records and the Linux `audit.log`). When an account on the web-server side (`www-data`, `apache`, `nginx` and so on) starts a shell or a reconnaissance command, or creates a script in the web root, within 5 seconds after the attack request, the confidence goes up (hypothesis → likely). It becomes "confirmed" only when, besides the shell, there is a fetch to the outside or a script created in the web root, and the request was answered with 2xx. Host records have no IP, so the link is by time only. If the clocks of the web log and the host records disagree, a pair is missed or an unrelated event is linked.
- (1.0.3) **Added: more log formats.** CRI container logs (Kubernetes, containerd), Cloudflare Logpush, Envoy / Istio (text and JSON), Google Cloud (`httpRequest` of load balancers and Cloud Run), the MySQL general log, and the MariaDB Audit Plugin log. For MySQL and MariaDB it looks at a success after failures and a login from an unfamiliar source.
- (1.0.3) **Added: the output of `gcloud logging read --format=json` (a JSON array) is read as it is.** So is JSON downloaded from the Cloud Logging console. An array that is cut short, or larger than 256 MiB, is reported as unreadable instead of silently becoming empty.
- (1.0.3) **Added: new commands and screen features.** `--export-roamswitch` (a draft of the Web Access Guard `web_guard.conf`), `accessscope fleet` (aggregation over several machines), `--print-schedule`, `--notify`, `suppress check`, `--what-if-suppress`, `--format brief-json`, and the step-by-step investigation `--llm-investigate`.
- (1.0.3) **Changed: with `--llm-investigate`, up to 6 times, as the LLM asks, more of what the diagnosis holds can be sent to the LLM service.** What can be sent: request and byte counts per IP (the top IPs by requests), a tally of URL paths that take numeric values, a record of stages that span several logs, and the log lines that serve as evidence (secrets are masked). IPs are neutralized or replaced by pseudonyms. Raw logs are neither kept nor sent. Without it, nothing changes (also written in EULA section 5-4).
- (1.0.3) **Fixed (found by checking against output written by real servers):**
  - MySQL general log: the line for a connection that failed authentication was counted as a success, so each refused attempt erased the failure count and "a success after failures" was never seen.
  - The time of MySQL `log_timestamps=SYSTEM` (`+09:00`) could not be read. A log that begins with the first-start initialization SQL was not recognized.
  - Container logs (CRI and Docker): a quiet container with few accesses and a long start-up output was not recognized as a web log.
  - Execution records: the first line of the file and the checkpoint lines were counted as unreadable lines.
  - Google Cloud: lines with `status` 0 (a connection with no response) or with no such field were discarded. They are read as 0.
  - Linux `audit.log`: a `/dev/tcp/` in a hexadecimal argument longer than 256 bytes, or in an argument split over several lines beyond about 4 KB, was missed, so an attack that should have been "confirmed" (shell, connection to the outside, 2xx) stayed at "likely".
- (1.0.3) The translations in the 10 languages were reviewed (the German word for confidence went from `Sicherheit`, which can also mean safety, to `Konfidenz`; the word for hit counts; the word order of format names; and others).
- (1.0.3) Checks: formats checked against real output are MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11.8 and 12.3, Envoy 1.34, containerd 2.1.3 CRI, RoamSwitch execution records, `audit.log` of Ubuntu 24.04 (auditd 3.1.2) and AlmaLinux 9.8 (auditd 3.1.5, SELinux enforcing), and `gcloud` output (Cloud Run). Cloudflare Logpush, Google Cloud load balancers and Podman were only compared with the vendors' documentation. In the evaluation data, 1146 of 1152 attacks are found, and the false positives of medium or higher are 0 of 404. The 641 workspace tests pass (3 ignored).
- (1.0.2) **Fixed: the sentence for a failed `--llm` analysis showed the internal notation `Some(1)` for the exit code.** It now shows only the number, "terminated by signal N" when it ended by a signal, and "terminated abnormally" when neither is known (10 languages). With an empty standard error only the state is returned, and the one line in the report drops the trailing punctuation of the reason before it is inserted, so it no longer ends in a doubled full stop. **Exit code 5 is unchanged.**
- (1.0.2) **Added: the check of values written by the LLM now includes IPv6 addresses, e-mail addresses and destinations (`IP:port`).** The check used to cover IPv4, domains, CVE numbers and hashes only, so an IPv6 address, an e-mail address or a C2-like destination that was not in the material went unnoticed. IPv6 addresses are compared as the same value despite case and abbreviation (`2001:DB8:0:0:0:0:0:1` and `2001:db8::1`). A destination is listed under "Values not in the collected material" only when the IP is in the material but the port does not appear near that IP. Counts, times, file paths and URL paths are not checked, because they do not match even when they come from the material (totals, rounding).
- (1.0.2) **Fixed: the error for reading the table of CVE rules showed `Some("…")`.** It now shows only the rule id, or "(no id)" when there is none.
- (1.0.2) **Changed: dependencies were updated** (rustls, rustls-webpki, tokio and others; advisories RUSTSEC-2026-0346 to 0349, 0382, 0383 and 0386). The advisory for `zip` (RUSTSEC-2026-0367 to 0369), a Windows-only dependency of `tauri-plugin-updater` used by the Mac in-app update, is removed by using a copy with `zip` 9.
- (1.0.2) The README now says that when used as root, the settings are read from `$HOME`.
- (1.0.2) Checks: the 522 workspace tests pass (2 ignored).
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: on a site that answers every URL with the same page, a flood of "suspected effect" 2xx responses pushed the severity to High.** Such a site varies by a few bytes (a token in the page), and only a size exactly equal to the usual page counted as "the usual page", so the wobble was counted as "unusual response". Two things were added:
  - A size close to the usual one (0.2% of the size, 2 to 12 bytes) counts as the same page. For responses under 500 bytes, a few bytes are a difference in content, so only an identical size is compared.
  - Even with no normal request in the log, 2xx responses of a similar size returned for 4 or more different file names (`.env`, `.git/config`, `phpinfo.php` and the like) count as that same page. One file read in several spellings (variants of `../../etc/passwd`) keeps a single file name, so it does not count.
  - On real access logs (3 sites, about 2 weeks), the "unusual 2xx" of "probing of secret and configuration files and admin pages" fell from 634 to 76, and the overall risk from 23 to 11.
- (1.0.1) **Changed: `203[.]0[.]113[.]9` and `example[.]com` in the LLM's text are shown with plain dots** (IPs and domains only; a `[.]` between letters or digits is restored, and a lone `[.]` in a regular-expression explanation is left as it is). The material is still given to the LLM in the defanged form. The list "Values not in the material" is also written with plain dots (report, terminal, JSON, window).
- **First release** (0.1.0): from web server access logs (Apache and Nginx common/combined, nginx / Caddy / Traefik JSON, gzip) and SSH authentication logs (sshd logs, and journald on Linux) it finds attack attempts and shows them with the evidence log lines, the MITRE ATT&CK technique ID and a confidence level (confirmed, likely, hypothesis). It is read-only: it never changes logs and never raises privileges. The analysis uses neither an LLM nor the network. The Mac app can update itself (Settings → Update); on Linux it is updated by the package.
- **What it finds** (0.1.0): SQL injection, path traversal, XSS, command and code injection, Log4Shell, Shellshock, template injection, SSRF, exploit paths of known vulnerabilities (Apache 2.4.49/50, PHPUnit, Laravel Ignition, Citrix, FortiOS, Spring4Shell, Confluence, Exchange and others), probing for secret files and admin pages, attack-tool User-Agents, mass attempts on non-existent paths, login brute force and distributed attempts (credential stuffing), web-shell-like access, chains from reconnaissance to attack, high-rate requests, SSH brute force, user-name probing, a success after failures and root password authentication, and instructions to an AI planted in logs. 0.2.0 added the User-Agents that HexStrike AI announces, httpx, CensysInspect and the api-recon bundled with ARTEX.
- **Fewer misses and false alarms** (0.2.0): measured on our own labelled test data (982 attack cases and 292 normal-traffic cases as of 0.2.0; the data grew during the work). Attack detection went from 83.3% to 99.1% overall, and from 15.7% to 98.6% on hard variants such as encoding and obfuscation. The share of normal traffic that raised a finding of medium severity or higher went from 30% to under 2% (5 of 292). These are figures on our data and do not guarantee the result on your logs.
- **Resistance to evasion** (0.2.0): before matching, it undoes repeated URL decoding (up to 3 times), alternative Unicode forms (fullwidth forms, overlong UTF-8, `%uXXXX`), SQL comments, `${IFS}` and nested Log4j lookups. The evidence line shown in a report stays the original log line. Numeric spellings of addresses in SSRF URLs (hex, decimal, octal, short forms) are read too. A line over 24 KiB is no longer dropped silently: it is analysed from its first and last parts, and the number of such lines is shown in the findings and the input list.
- **A CVE rule pack** (0.2.0): exploit attempts that are recognisable from the request line and appear in CISA's KEV (the catalog of known exploited vulnerabilities) are matched against a data table with sources: 64 rules covering 71 CVEs, dated 2026-10-08. The date and rule count appear at the top of the report, in the JSON and in `--version`, and a finding lists the CVEs it matched. A match means the request had the shape of an exploit; it does not mean the attempt succeeded.
- **Detections over time windows** (0.2.0): login brute force (windows of 1 hour, 1 minute and 14 days), login spraying, SSH spraying (`ssh_spray`), distributed probing by IPs that mostly get 404, and site-wide floods (`web_flood_dist`). A success after SSH failures is counted in the preceding 1-hour window too. When sshd writes two lines for one invalid-user attempt, it is counted once.
- **Judging the effect of an attack** (0.1.0, 0.2.0): an HTTP 200 is not treated as success (0.1.0). If a 2xx response to an attack-looking request is the same size as the normal page, it is reported as "the payload was probably ignored" and does not raise the severity. The overall risk (0 to 100) is weighted per kind of finding, so the number of attacking IPs does not inflate it. In 0.2.0 the response size alone is no longer used: a 2xx to an attack-looking request is rated high, medium or low by where the payload sits and who sent it (a script or a browser); for example, a browser sending it in a search parameter is medium / hypothesis. If normal responses from the same path already come in 3 or more sizes, a different size is not treated as evidence. No rule removes a finding; the reason for a lowered severity or confidence is added to the detail as a sentence.
- **Kinds of logs** (0.2.0): more logs can be read: authentication logs of sudo, su, PAM and login, fail2ban, HAProxy (HTTP and TCP), AWS ALB and CloudFront, Postfix and Dovecot, and MySQL, MariaDB and PostgreSQL, with 16 kinds of findings added. An IP that fail2ban already banned is noted on the other findings. Checked against logs captured on real Debian 12, Rocky Linux 9, HAProxy, MariaDB, MySQL and PostgreSQL. sshd logs are read from the macOS unified log (a fixed command, `/usr/bin/log show`, run by absolute path, without a shell and with an empty environment; only members of the admin group can read it). Docker container logs can be read too (on Linux, `json-file` log files are also searched; `docker logs` is used only when you choose "Also check Docker containers" in the window and confirm the commands and targets shown, or when you pass `--docker` on the command line; the only commands that can be built are `logs` and `ps`, environment variables such as `DOCKER_HOST` are not inherited and the socket is pinned to this machine, so a remote Docker is never contacted).
- **Log discovery** (0.1.0, 0.2.0): besides the standard locations it checks the places named by nginx, Apache, Caddy and Traefik configs (including rotated files) and, on Linux, journald (0.1.0). Optionally an LLM is given only facts about the environment (names of running servers, log locations named in configs, file names such as under `/var/log`; never file contents) and suggests missing locations. A suggestion is shown only after it has been confirmed to be readable as a log, and nothing is read until you choose to add it. 0.2.0 shows a result list: the verdict for each of the standard locations, config files, logs named in configs, journald, the unified log, Docker and LLM suggestions (a log that a config names but that does not exist is shown as such instead of being dropped silently, and when nothing is found the reason is given in a sentence). The list of found logs is saved (0600, up to 200 entries) and restored at the next start with the time of the last search, and all entries are checked again. The "Review what will be sent" button was removed from "Find logs with an LLM" in the window (the explanation of what is sent stays; `discover --show-prompt` on the command line remains, and the review feature in the LLM analysis panel is unchanged).
- **Analysis by an LLM** (0.1.0, 0.2.0): optional, off by default. Only a summary and a few evidence log lines are passed to an LLM (claude, opencode, codex, agy) to write a report. Raw logs are not sent; secrets are masked and IPs are defanged (or pseudonymized). `--show-prompt` shows exactly what would be sent. Logs are attacker-controlled, so text that looks like instructions to an AI is reported as a finding and the LLM output is treated as an untrusted quotation. It is not run as root. When enabled, a summary can be sent to an external service through the LLM command you chose (0.1.0). In 0.2.0 the instructions to the LLM say how to reach a conclusion on each finding: one of "confirmed", "can be ruled out (with the reason taken from facts)" or "undetermined", and something that could not be confirmed is never written as "no problem".
- **Suppression, comparison and export** (0.2.0): `--trust-forwarded-for` (off by default) takes the client IP from X-Forwarded-For (rightmost or leftmost) for logs behind a reverse proxy; the header can be forged, so it is not used by default, and it can also be chosen under "Analysis" in Settings. Suppression rules are a TOML file that names IPs, User-Agents, paths or finding kinds; a reason is mandatory and an expiry (`expires`) can be set; a high-severity finding is never hidden unless `allow_high = true`; suppressed findings are not thrown away, their count and reasons appear in the report (`--show-suppressed` lists them); `suppress.toml` in the settings folder is read automatically, and in the window you can add an entry from a finding's IP with "Suppress this IP…". Verified crawlers are told apart by checking IPs against the ranges the vendors publish officially (a snapshot from 2026-10-07; no network access); a verified crawler is left out of the reconnaissance, enumeration, scanner, high-rate and error findings and is counted instead, and a client that claims to be a crawler but comes from outside the range is reported as `web_fake_crawler` (low). For comparison with the previous run, `--state` and `--since-last` store only fingerprints of findings (a hash of kind, IP and classification) with counts and times, and split the result into new, continuing and gone since last time; with `--new-only` and `--fail-on`, the exit code is 3 only when there is a new finding; the state file is 0600 and discarded after 90 days. `--export-blocklist` writes a blocklist for fail2ban, nftables, ipset, nginx, Apache or CSV (it only writes: nothing is run and no traffic is blocked; IPs of official crawler ranges, IPs covered by suppression rules and private or documentation ranges are left out, and the header records the generation date, source logs, criteria, count and a warning). `--format` also offers CSV (with a leading formula character neutralised), SARIF 2.1.0 and JSON (the JSON carries `schema_version` and follows a versioned specification). The window can export too ("Export…").
- **Screens and languages** (0.1.0, 0.2.0): the screens and the command line are in 10 languages (the texts added in 0.2.0 too; translations are machine-made and have not been reviewed by native speakers). In 0.2.0 the input log list shows the number of long lines, the number of lines whose client IP came from X-Forwarded-For, and a note for formats that have no header columns; the top of the results shows the CVE rule pack date and rule count and the date of the crawler IP ranges; Settings gained "Analysis".
- **Activity across logs, logins from unfamiliar sources, changes from the usual** (0.3.0): the activity of one address that appears in two or more logs is shown as one time-ordered chain (`actor_chain`; the only link is the identical address, and the text says it is not proof of the same person or tool). A successful login from a source that had no successful login in the previous 30 days, for an account with an established baseline, is reported too (`ssh_new_source_login` and the like; low, hypothesis; medium when failures came first or the address appears in other logs). Changes of the recent period against the earlier part of the same log are reported (`web_new_ua`, `web_new_path`, `web_path_shift`; always low, hypothesis; `--baseline-period`, `--state` and `--since-last`). Suppression rules gained `user` (only for the three unfamiliar-source login kinds). When Docker cannot be used, the reason is shown. The "diagnose logs" screen was reorganised and technical terms were removed from the Docker explanation.
- **Measured effect** (0.3.0): the thresholds are judgement calls checked on our own evaluation data and were not tuned on real traffic. Attack recall stayed at 99.1% (1006 of 1015) and benign cases with a medium or higher finding were 5 of 312. Analysing 3.3 million lines took about 6% more time and about 1% more memory. Integration tests confirm that the same log always gives the same result. The new text is in 10 languages (based on machine translation).
- **Korean and Chinese terms unified** (0.3.1): the words for "severity" and "confidence" were aligned in the Korean and Chinese windows and reports. Korean confidence is now "확신도" (it was confusable with "신뢰" of trusted proxies). In Simplified and Traditional Chinese, "严重度/嚴重度" became "严重程度/嚴重程度", the confidence level "有力" became "较可能/較可能", and the severity "中等" became "中". What is detected and how it is judged is unchanged.
- (0.3.2) **Changed: the terminal output was tidied.** It no longer lists every log it read one by one. It counts them per kind (for example "Read 47 logs (Web access log 38, ...)") and names only the logs that could not be read. Add `--list-sources` to list them all. Findings that may have succeeded (a login after brute force, a web-shell-like request that got a success response, a login from an unfamiliar source) are named by number on a "Check first" line above the list. A note that applied to several findings is now printed once as a footnote (※1) at the end. Findings of the same attack chain are listed under one heading, one line per IP. The Markdown report (`--report`) opens with a table of all findings (number, severity, confidence, title, count) and the "Check first" line, and a long list of logs it read is counted per kind in the same way. In Japanese, the confidence levels are now shown as 高 / 中 / 低 (high / medium / low) instead of the old 確定 / 有力 / 仮説, which were unusual and hard to understand. The heading reads "[深刻度 高/確度 低]" (severity high / confidence low), and the output carries a one-line explanation of what confidence means. The labels in the other languages are unchanged. What is detected and how it is judged are unchanged. The text is in 10 languages.
- (0.3.3) **New: clients that behave very differently from the other visitors** (`web_ml_outlier`). It finds clients that no signature explains but that behave very differently from the other visitors of the same log: a scraper that keeps walking articles in order, a client walking the IDs of `/api/orders/N`, a client hammering a search API. For each client with at least 30 requests it computes 14 features (peak requests per minute, number of different paths, share of static files, share of requests with a Referer, how regular the gaps between requests are, and so on), compares them with the median and the spread of the same log, and reports only clients that are far away in two or more unrelated ways. A client that is extreme in only one way is not reported. There is no trained model, no network use, and the same log always gives the same result. The text says what differs, with the client's value and the usual value. Severity is always low and confidence a hypothesis (monitoring tools, your own app's API clients and people saving a site with `wget` look the same; these IPs are never put into the blocklist). Not candidates: clients that already have a reason (a signature hit, a scanner, 404 enumeration, login guessing, a flood), known crawlers and clients that call themselves a bot (`bot`, `crawler`, `spider`, `+http`), and clients that only repeat a few paths at a steady pace (monitors, health checks, feed fetches). If fewer than 30 clients can be compared, nothing is said and the report says why (`behavior` in the JSON). Limit: a scraper that behaves like a browser is not found (it fetches the static files, sends a Referer and keeps human-like gaps). Measured on simulated logs that we wrote (not real traffic): on 40 logs not used for tuning, all 114 unwanted automated clients were reported and none of 39,057 human visitors. It is weaker on small sites of about 100 visitors (31 of 41 fast scrapers, 15 of 39 slow ones). The existing evaluation data is unchanged (attack recall 1006/1015, 5 of 312 benign cases with a medium or higher finding). Time on 3.3 million lines is unchanged; memory grows by about 40 MB (310 MB to 351 MB). An Isolation Forest (a forest of outlier-finding trees) was also tried and not adopted because it did not reduce false reports. The new text is in 10 languages (based on machine translation; not reviewed by native speakers).
- (0.3.4) **Changed: the command line can now run LLM analysis as root too** (like PersonalSOC). It used to refuse `--llm` as root. `sudo accessscope scan --llm NAME` works. **What an LLM reads is written by attackers (log lines, user names, URLs). `agy` and `codex` cannot be stopped from running commands, so as root they run with root's rights** (`accessscope llms` shows the tool-lock state of each LLM). To keep the LLM away from root, run the diagnosis as root with `--show-prompt` (it sends nothing and prints the whole text for the LLM) and hand that text to the LLM as an unprivileged user. The window app still does not run LLM analysis as root. Diagnosis without `--llm` already worked as root.
- (0.3.4) **Changed: the terminal output is now Markdown tables.** In this order: an overview table (overall risk, logs read, summary lines), "Check first", the findings table (number, severity, confidence, finding, count, main sources), a table of multi-stage attack chains (one row per IP) and a table of notes. Chains stay in one table however many there are. The evidence log lines and the full explanation of each finding are not in the list; they are in the Markdown report (`--report FILE`). `--plain` prints the previous sentence-style list. The window app and the Markdown report are unchanged. The table headings and similar text are in 10 languages (based on machine translation; not reviewed by native speakers). What is detected and how it is judged is unchanged.
- (0.3.5) **Added: detection of XSS in headers and of out-of-band (OAST) callback hosts.** Misses in the XSS detection were measured against publicly documented techniques and closed. The 229 payloads of an XSS testing tool were run through six encodings and through the User-Agent and Referer headers. In the query (as is, URL, double URL, HTML entity encoding) detection went from 218/229 to 229/229; in User-Agent and Referer from 1/229 to 229/229 (XSS used to be left out of the header detection). Obfuscated calls after leaving a string (`window['ale'+'rt']`, `(alert)(1)`, `eval(atob(..))` and so on) and calling `alert` with a template literal in backticks were added. For headers, the body patterns are not applied; only a strict check is made: a tag, a `javascript:` call, a call after an event attribute, or a bare `alert(1)` shape. Something that is only a search term in the browser's Referer is lowered to a low-confidence hypothesis.
- (0.3.5) **Added: a new kind, `web_oast`.** It finds callback hosts of interactsh (`oast.pro`, `oast.live`, `oast.site`, `oast.online`, `oast.fun`, `oast.me`, `interact.sh`) and Burp Collaborator (`oastify.com`, `burpcollaborator.net`). A random subdomain is required, and paths such as `/scripts/interact.sh` are not matched. In a header it is reported as `web_header_attack(oast)`.
- (0.3.5) **Fixed: one false positive.** A parameter whose name starts with `on` and whose value is only a function name (a normal script URL setting), such as `onload=onloadCallback`, was reported as `web_xss` at high.
- (0.3.5) 53 cases (`xss_ext.jsonl`) were added to the evaluation data: attacks and benign look-alikes. The known gaps of the existing evaluation (14) and the false positives on normal traffic did not increase.
- (0.3.6) **Changed: for logs behind a reverse proxy or a Docker gateway, the real client is now decided automatically.** Only for lines whose recorded source is an internal address (loopback, private, link-local, CGNAT), `X-Forwarded-For` is read from the right, and the first address that is not a trusted relay becomes the client (the same idea as nginx's `real_ip_recursive`). Before, 127.0.0.1 and 172.x.0.1 were listed as attack sources (found while diagnosing a production Ubuntu server). It is not applied to lines that came directly from outside. If every value is an internal address (a request from the host itself), the original address stays. The number of lines resolved this way appears in the report. To turn it off, use `--trust-forwarded-for=off` (in the window, choose "Do not use" under "Analysis" in Settings; the choices are "Automatic (default)", "Do not use", "From the right" and "Leftmost").
- (0.3.6) **Changed: an IP decided from a header is not treated as a verified crawler.** This prevents an attacker from writing a crawler's official IP in `X-Forwarded-For` and calling itself `Googlebot` to escape the reconnaissance and high-rate findings. It is the same with the automatic rule and with `--trust-forwarded-for`. A line that came directly from an official IP is verified as before.
- (0.3.6) **Fixed: internal addresses no longer form a "multi-stage attack chain".** An internal address is a row that merges many clients, not the action of one attacker (on a real server, 127.0.0.1 and the Docker gateway had become chains rated high).
- (0.3.6) **Fixed: chains with no sign of success are now rated low, and the list shows at most 10.** On a real server 74 chains were listed and buried the point. The rest is given as a count ("N more"), and all of them are in `--report` and the JSON. A chain with a suspected success or a web shell is still rated high, and all of them are shown.
- (0.3.6) **Fixed: a redirect (301, 307 and so on) to an uploaded script is no longer counted as a web shell being used.** A request that only got a redirect had made a chain high (web shell). A 200 response is still counted as before.
- (0.3.6) **Fixed: the Apache logs on openSUSE are now found** (`/var/log/apache2/access_log` and the config `/etc/apache2/httpd.conf`).
- (0.3.6) Verified by reading real Apache and nginx logs in containers of Fedora, Rocky Linux 9, openSUSE Leap 15.6, Arch and Debian 13 (on Fedora, with nginx in two tiers, the automatic resolution of `X-Forwarded-For` too).
- **Settings tidy-up and LLM analysis as root** (0.3.7): the settings tabs now follow the same order and names as PathScope and PersonalSOC (tool-specific tabs first, then "LLM integration", "Display language" and "Updates"). The desktop app can run the LLM analysis as root as well, treated the same as on the command line (the settings show a notice recommending an unprivileged user). The `--trust-forwarded-for` note is now one line for the whole run instead of one per log read.
- **Reporting what could not be read** (0.3.8): when not all of a log could be read (unreadable logs, a cut at the size limit, over-long lines, lines skipped outside the period, lines that could not be parsed, command input that ended early), what was missed and how much is summarised under the overview as the part that is not visible. "No findings" with part unread now reads "no findings in what was checked", and JSON, CSV and SARIF carry the same numbers. Also fixed: every `.gz` log shown as only partly read, newer logs dropped in a folder of more than 500, and command input cut short shown as normal.
- (0.4.0) **Added: the values the LLM writes are checked against what it was given.** An IP address, domain, CVE ID or hash in the LLM's text that is not in the summary and evidence lines it received is shown, in the LLM section of the report and on screen, under "Values not found in the collected material", as a possible invention. Only the strings are compared; the LLM is not asked.
- (0.4.0) **Added: 4 CVE rules (68 rules and 73 CVEs in total).** One is the Apache Tomcat partial PUT (CVE-2025-24813): a PUT of a `.session` file to a writable default servlet. Ordinary REST shapes such as `PUT /name/session` are not matched. The other three are for the GitLab repository API (CVE-2026-85706): `file.path` passed directly in the query, `repository` or `commits` encoded as `%72` or `%63` to get around route matching, and the `commits/` and `commits.json` endings. A normal `POST .../repository/commits` and `GET` listings are not flagged. The rules carry the date 2026-10-19. **Limit**: only the request shape in the log is checked. The `Content-Range` header and the cookie that follow a Tomcat attack do not appear in the log, so success cannot be told.
- (0.4.1) **Added: identifier walking (`web_id_enum`) is detected**. Numbers such as receipt or order numbers swapped in one after another at the same place are found by the match between the order of arrival and the size of the values, whatever the IP. A walk split over many IPs is found too (3 or more IPs: medium, likely; fewer: low, hypothesis). Scattered values, one value asked many times and ordinary browsing are not flagged. Verified crawlers and clients that declare automation are skipped.
- **The check of the LLM's values also appears in the terminal** (0.4.2): IPs, domains, CVE IDs and hashes that are not in the material appear right after the LLM's text in the terminal output (`--plain` too), in the same list as in the report. `--format json` carries them in the new key `llm.ungrounded`. Nothing is shown when there are no such values.
- **Help fix** (0.4.3): the help of `accessscope` said "LLM analysis cannot run as root"; it now matches the behavior (since 0.3.4 the command line runs as root too, and the LLM then runs with root's rights). The `--json` line now says it is the same as `--format json` (10 languages).
- **Changed: when the `--llm` analysis fails, the exit code is now 5. This is why the version is 1.0.0.** Before, a failed LLM only wrote to standard error, appeared neither in the report nor in the exit code, and the exit code was 0 when nothing was detected. The failure is now written in the report (one line in the Markdown and terminal output, `llm.failed` in JSON) and the exit code is 5. A detection (3) and `--fail-on-partial` (4) take priority. A script or CI that treated an LLM failure as exit code 0 must now handle 5. PersonalSOC ends with 1 on the same failure. (1.0.0)
- **Fixed: `--fail-on` quietly exited with 0 even when some logs could not be read.** The `--fail-on` verdict covers only what could be read. When some part could not be examined (logs that could not be read, a cut-off at the size limit, lines that were too long, lines skipped outside the period, lines that could not be parsed), it still exits with 0 if nothing was detected, and now prints a notice on standard error. (1.0.0)
- **Added: `--fail-on-partial`.** Together with `--fail-on`, it ends with exit code 4 when some part could not be examined (a detection still gives 3 first). It is not the default, because the parts that could not be examined include logs outside the period and long lines, so making it the default would break existing CI. (1.0.0)
- **Exit codes**: 0 no problem, 1 no readable log or output could not be written, 2 wrong usage, 3 `--fail-on` matched, 4 `--fail-on-partial` and some part could not be examined, 5 the `--llm` analysis failed. When two or more apply, the smaller number wins (3 and 4 take priority over 5). The usage text (10 languages) and the README list the flags and the exit codes. (1.0.0)
- Verification: 504 workspace tests pass and 1 fails (2 ignored). The failure predates this change; `timeout_kills_grandchildren` is a problem of the container environment. (1.0.0)

## PathScope

### 1.0.6

- **Fixed: `--version` of `pathscope-diff` and `pathscope-fleet` was read as the name of a file to compare, so they printed the usage and exited with code 2.** They now print "<name> <version>" and exit with code 0.
- Checks: 420 workspace tests pass (Mac; 0 failed; 4 ignored).

### 1.0.5

- **Fixed: writing to a closed pipe printed a panic message.** It now exits with code 141 without it.
- **Fixed: English, German, French, Spanish, Italian and Portuguese use the form that matches the number.**
- **Changed: new exports and `pathscope-propose-rule` drafts are written readable by the owner only (0600)** (existing files keep their mode).
- **Fixed: opening the history showed the notes in the language they were saved in.** They are shown in the current language.
- **Fixed: a broken saved scan (`facts.json`) looked as if nothing had been saved.** It now says "cannot be loaded: … this does not mean there is none", and the analysis notes say so too. At the next save, the broken file is kept as `facts.json.corrupt-…`, the newest 3.
- **Changed: the text sent to the LLM in the second-stage investigation (reading configuration files) is in English whatever the display language.** The reasons and results shown on screen stay in the display language.

### 1.0.4

- **Changed: when run with sudo without `--start`, the starting point is the user who ran sudo, and it is printed to standard error.**

### 1.0.3

- **Fixed: the finding about write access to `/Applications` on macOS suggested a `sudo chown` fix that does not work.** `/Applications` is writable by the admin group by default on macOS and is protected by macOS, so `sudo chown` fails with "Operation not permitted". It no longer offers a command that cannot run; it advises using a standard user day to day and doing administrator work from a separate administrator account (10 languages; the window uses the dedicated text under the same condition).
- Checks: 395 workspace tests pass (on a Mac). `cargo audit` reports 0 vulnerabilities.

### 0.1.0 - 1.0.2 (summarized)

The contents of these releases, grouped by theme. The full text of each entry is in this file's git history.

- (1.0.2) **Changed: the `kvm` group is no longer treated as a basis for a path to root.** Being able to open `/dev/kvm` is not root privilege in itself.
- (1.0.2) **Changed: of the executables with `cap_setuid`, only the interpreters listed in GTFOBins are counted as able to become root.** Ordinary tools such as `newuidmap` were being reported as "critical".
- (1.0.2) **Changed: what `--export-roamswitch` writes is now Falco rules** (for RoamSwitch Server). Check them and put them in `/etc/falco/rules.d/` yourself; they are not applied automatically.
- (1.0.2) **Added: multi-step privilege escalation (user → user → root) is found.**
- (1.0.2) **Added: configuration parsing was widened.** Deterministic parsers for sudoers, cron and doas, systemd `EnvironmentFile`, and tracking of macOS dylibs and `@rpath`.
- (1.0.2) **Added: kernel conditions** (`unprivileged_bpf`, `io_uring`, kernel modules) **are reflected in the match against known vulnerabilities.**
- (1.0.2) **Added: `--check-agent-users`, credentials in other users' home directories, and uid detection of services that root listens on.**
- (1.0.2) **Added: the output formats `--format=markdown|plain|json|sarif`, and the command in 10 languages.**
- (1.0.2) **Fixed: configuration files that could not be read are recorded as "range that could not be read" instead of being skipped silently.** `/etc/sudoers` that only root can read, and content that is not UTF-8, were skipped, so `NOPASSWD` and the like were not read yet "no path" could be taken as safe.
- (1.0.2) **Fixed: a Prolog lexer was added to the verifier.** It closes a way past the deny list by mixing up quotation marks.
- (1.0.2) **Fixed: the Falco rules it writes were checked against a real Falco and corrected** (reproducible with `packaging/falco/live-check.sh`).
  - `mv` fired twice for one operation (after the failing `RENAME_NOREPLACE` it retries). Only a successful `rename` fires now.
  - A failed `execve` during the PATH search also fired. Only a successful `execve` fires now.
  - Every read of `/etc/shadow` by `sudo`, `su`, `sshd`, `passwd`, `chage` and `unix_chkpwd` fired (15 of 17 ordinary uses). The list of authentication tools is excluded. Another user's key is excluded when the owner reads it.
  - An operation reaching `/run/docker.sock` through systemd socket activation was missed. Both `/run` and `/var/run` are watched. Connections by root are excluded.
  - A write through a symbolic link and `chmod` are not visible to a Falco rule (written in `docs/DESIGN.md`).
- (1.0.2) The translations in the 10 languages were reviewed (missing parts of the Spanish and Portuguese usage text, the German "root listens" that read as "eavesdrops", and others).
- (1.0.2) Checks: the 328 workspace tests pass (on a Mac, without the window app). The Falco rules were checked on Ubuntu 24.04 (kernel 6.8, Falco 0.39.2): execution, write, replace, read and socket connection fire as intended, and ordinary use does not fire them. On Ubuntu 26.04 (kernel 7.0, Falco 0.45.1) it goes as far as loading, and the read and socket rules firing. The list of authentication tools was checked on a real Ubuntu 26.04 machine: on a public-key login only `sshd-session` and `unix_chkpwd` open `/etc/shadow`, and with `sudo-rs` password authentication it is `unix_chkpwd` that opens it.
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: when root ran PathScope with `--start=<user>`, the paths through that user's groups were not shown.** Group membership was looked up with the groups of the running process itself, so `sudo pathscope --start=admin` could not see admin's `docker` group (the same admin running it as a normal user does). When the start user differs from the running user, the groups of that user are looked up (Linux, macOS). A user that does not exist is not given the running user's groups; it is recorded as a range that could not be read.
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: where `$USER` is empty (a systemd system service, a cron with a cleared environment), the start point became `unknown`.** The running user is now taken from the uid and used as the start point.
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: the Mac collector read root's `LaunchAgents` and `TCC.db` even when root ran it with `--start=admin`.** It now reads the home of the `--start` user (a user that cannot be resolved is not replaced by root's home).
- (1.0.1) **Added: `pathscope-diff` (report comparison) and `pathscope-fleet` (aggregation of many hosts) are now installed with Linux (deb, rpm, tarball) and in the Mac PathScope.app.** The README listed them as CLIs but the packages did not carry them. Both print their usage with `--help` and exit with code 0 (before, they exited with 2 as with no arguments).
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: a failure of the notification command (`notify-send`, `osascript`) is written to the standard error** (it could not start, the exit code was not 0, it ended by a signal; in a scheduled run it stays in the launchd or systemd log). The exit code is unchanged.
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: the sentence for a failed LLM showed `Some(1)` and "-".** It now shows only the number for the exit code, the signal number for a signal, and "terminated abnormally" when neither is known (10 languages).
- (1.0.1) **Added: the check of values written by the LLM now includes IPv6 addresses, e-mail addresses and destinations (`IP:port`).** The details are the same as in AccessScope 1.0.2.
- (1.0.1) **Changed: make the baseline file with the same privilege and the same `--start` as the scheduled run** (written in the README). Reports with different start points are not compared and give exit code 2.
- (1.0.1) **Changed: dependencies were updated** (rustls, rustls-webpki, tokio, num-modular and others; the `zip` advisory is removed with a `zip` 9 copy). The README now says that when used as root, the settings are read from `$HOME`.
- (1.0.1) Checks: 289 workspace tests pass (3 ignored; the Mac-only `collector-macos` is not run on Linux).
- **The version is now 1.0.0** (1.0.0): it moved to 1.0.0 together with Sensor, PersonalSOC and AccessScope.
- **A scheduled check that stopped working without a sound** (1.0.0): with `--baseline --fail-on=new --notify`, when the baseline file could not be read, or could not be compared because the OS, the starting point or the machine differed, it only returned exit code 2 and wrote to standard error. With `--notify` it now notifies "PathScope's scheduled check is not running: reason" (the reason is a fixed sentence only).
- **Comparing with a baseline collected with different rights** (1.0.0): if you made the baseline as root and ran the schedule as an ordinary user, an increase in paths that the ordinary user cannot see went unnoticed. The report now records the rights it was collected with, and when the baseline and the current run differ it warns on standard error before the comparison (when the baseline is root and the current run is an ordinary user, it also notifies; `pathscope-diff` gives the same warning). Make the baseline file with the same rights as the scheduled run (root or an ordinary user). The GUI screen "compare two history entries" does not have this warning.
- **The GUI's automatic save to the history** (1.0.0): it said nothing when it could not save, for example when the database could not be opened or the save failed. The result's "assumptions of the analysis" now says "could not be saved to the history: reason".
- **Added: PathScope can now be bundled in RoamSwitch's Linux packages (deb, rpm and tarball) on Linux.** It installs a launcher entry and icons so that it opens from the application menu, the `pathscope` command, the `pathscope-gui` window and the Apache-2.0 texts. An install made by a package does not use Settings > Update; it says that updates come with that package's updates. (0.1.4)
- **Fixed: on Linux (WebKitGTK) in dark mode, a closed drop-down kept a white background and its text was unreadable.** (0.1.4)
- **Fixed: depending on the startup order, the text in the update section could stay as a raw key name.** It could happen when the update information arrived before the text catalog was loaded (on both Mac and Linux). (0.1.4)
- **Changed: even with "unrestricted", private key files and API key or password values are not sent.** Until now, choosing "unrestricted" turned off both the replacement of values and the exclusion of files such as private keys. From now on, the following are kept whatever the setting. Private key files (a renamed key, or a key pasted into a config file, is found by its content, `-----BEGIN … PRIVATE KEY-----`). API keys in well-known formats (AWS, GitHub, Slack, OpenAI/Anthropic, Google, GitLab, npm, Stripe, JWT, `Bearer`), passwords inside URLs, and values whose name means a password or key (`name=value`, `name: value`, JSON, `--name value`) are replaced with `[REDACTED]`. What "unrestricted" still turns off is the exclusion of things like password hashes and `.env`, and the guess that treats long alphanumeric strings as secrets. Because this works by names and shapes, some secrets can still be missed. （0.1.2）
- **Fixed: the German label for "unrestricted" is now "Uneingeschränkt".** The earlier "Unbegrenzt" reads as "unlimited in quantity". The settings screen now matches the wording on the website and in the EULA. （0.1.3）
- **Fixed: even in standard mode, a renamed private key or a key pasted into a config file is no longer sent.** Files were excluded only by name and location, so a key with a name such as `backup.conf` could have part of its content sent (the replacement of long alphanumeric strings does not apply to lines that contain `/`). JSON `"password": "…"` and tokens that contain `.`, such as JWTs, are now replaced too. （0.1.2）
- **Fixed: with "unrestricted", the protection against instructions planted in config files (removing invisible characters, defusing the data fence marks) was also turned off.** It is now always applied, whatever the setting. （0.1.2）
- **Fixed: the result of "Fix everything?" is shown in plain sentences instead of internal wording.** （0.1.2）
- **Added: have an LLM read your config files to find easily missed risks (LLM assist).** Files such as sudoers, cron and scripts are written in many different styles, so a program alone cannot always read them. The LLM looks for entries that involve root privileges (for example, a sudo setting that needs no password, or a file that root runs periodically). Before anything it finds is used in the analysis, PathScope checks that the line really exists in the original file. Before sending, you confirm which LLM will receive how many files. Values that look like passwords or keys are replaced with `[REDACTED]`, and files such as private keys are not sent (for a local LLM whose content never leaves your machine, you can choose "unrestricted" in Settings). Files that could not be read or were not sent are listed as "unchecked" with the reason. The LLM integration is still off by default. (0.1.1)
- **Added: the app is available in 10 languages** (Japanese, English, Korean, Simplified Chinese, Traditional Chinese, German, French, Spanish, Italian, Portuguese). It follows the OS language and can be changed in Settings → Language. The command line supports Japanese and English (`--lang=ja|en`). The translations started as machine translations and have not been reviewed by native speakers. The macOS screen names (System Settings, Privacy & Security, Full Disk Access) match the wording macOS itself uses in each language. (0.1.1)
- **Added: in-app updates on Linux.** The update is installed only after its signature is verified; updates with a wrong signature or a tampered file are refused. The Linux package (.deb) in this release is for delivering in-app updates; it is not yet offered as an installer. (0.1.1)
- **Fixed: the macOS privacy settings data (TCC.db) is now read correctly from real data**, including apps that are allowed by bundle ID. Commands in the suggested fixes are safely quoted even when the path contains spaces or symbols. (0.1.1)
- **Changed: the wording of the app and the command line is easier to understand** (the reason for each step is shown as a sentence instead of an internal name; "choke point" became "fix point", and so on). Settings is organized into tabs by function, overlapping text in the diagram is fixed, and a confirmation that explains the reason appears before opening the Full Disk Access settings. (0.1.1)
- **First release.** Bundled in the Mac 1.10.19 DMG. It analyzes, read-only, the paths by which privileges can be escalated on this machine (the routes to root or to sensitive data) and shows which places to fix first. (0.1.0)
- **Matching installed software against known vulnerabilities (0.1.5)**: Added: it checks the versions of installed software against known vulnerabilities. It compares the versions of packages, the running kernel and macOS with known vulnerabilities that were exploited in real attacks (50 entries from the CISA KEV catalog that let a regular user become root), and adds the ones that are not yet fixed to the paths to root. There is one path per package, the unit you fix, listing the matching CVE numbers. The suggested fix is an update command (apt, dnf, zypper, pacman, apk or softwareupdate); nothing is run automatically. "Fix this?" lets you analyze the result of updating without changing anything. Supported environments: macOS, and on Linux the releases of Debian, Ubuntu, RHEL-compatible systems (including AlmaLinux and Rocky Linux), SUSE (SLES and openSUSE Leap), Arch and Alpine for which fixed-version data exists. Releases without data (Debian 11 and older, Fedora, openSUSE Tumbleweed, derivatives other than those above) are shown as "unchecked", not as "no problem". What it does not do: it is not a complete vulnerability scan. Only entries in the KEV catalog that let a local user gain privileges are covered; vulnerabilities attacked remotely and application packages are not checked. It judges by version only, so it does not check whether the affected component is actually in use. For entries that need an extra condition (a SUID executable, or unprivileged user namespaces for regular users), a path appears only when the condition is met. For the kernel, only the running one is checked, not every installed kernel. The data is bundled with the app. Nothing is looked up over the network during an analysis. The result shows the date of the data, and warns when it is more than 45 days old. New data arrives with app updates. The screens and the command line were added in 10 languages (the translations are based on machine translation and have not been reviewed by native speakers).
- **Added: when PathScope itself is the subject of a finding, an explanation is added** (macOS). The finding that an app with Full Disk Access can be overwritten by an ordinary user also applies to PathScope itself, which asks you to allow it so that it can read the privacy settings data (TCC.db). The finding is not hidden: it is marked as being about PathScope itself, with what to do (remove the permission in System Settings once the reading is done; making root the owner also works, but the in-app update then asks for the administrator password and the owner may revert after an update). It is shown in both the app and the command line. The text was added in 10 languages (the translations are based on machine translation and have not been checked by native speakers). (0.1.6)
- (0.1.7) **Updated: the known-vulnerability data is now the CISA KEV catalog 2026.10.04 (data generated 2026-10-05).** This is the data used by the check of installed software versions against known vulnerabilities (0.1.5); until now it was the 2026.10.02 catalog (generated 2026-10-04). The vulnerability entries in the data are the same as before; only the catalog version and the generation date changed. The data date shown in the result is newer, which gives more time before the notice that appears when the data is over 45 days old. The data is bundled with the app, and nothing is looked up over the network during an analysis. There are no other changes.
- (0.1.8) **New: the local privilege escalation CVE data now includes CVEs that are easy to exploit even if they are not in KEV.** The selection widened from "listed in KEV" to "listed in KEV, or EPSS (the predicted probability of exploitation) was 0.5 or more at any point in the last 30 days". EPSS comes day by day from FIRST's official API. It selects by the maximum in the 30-day window, not the current value, because the EPSS of a new CVE is high right after publication and drops sharply within weeks (on 2026-09-24, the two Dirty Frag CVEs also fell from the 0.9s to the 0.02s). The existing conditions (Linux kernel, sudo and so on; local, high confidentiality and integrity, privileges not HIGH in NVD) apply to CVEs entering through EPSS as well. The 50 existing KEV-based entries were not reduced. Three were added (CVE-2023-22809 sudoedit, CVE-2026-43284 and CVE-2026-43500 Dirty Frag). Dirty Frag can be matched with real fixed-version information from Debian, Ubuntu, RHEL and SUSE. A CVE added only through EPSS does not widen the "confirmed distributions and versions" coverage. The wording on screen no longer says flatly "exploited in a real attack"; it now says "or is likely to be exploited" (10 languages; other than Japanese and English they are machine translations without native review).
- (0.1.8) **New: a CVE once added through EPSS stays even if its EPSS falls.** If a CVE whose EPSS stayed low after the window dropped out, it would vanish before kernel updates had spread, and a machine that has not been fixed would look "fine" (Dirty Frag would have disappeared in the update of the week of 2026-10-26). A CVE leaves only when you put it in the exclusion list, when NVD's scoring changes so that it no longer meets the conditions, or when it moves because it was listed in KEV. If NVD or a distribution's data merely could not be fetched, the previous entry stays. There is no expiry. A kept entry carries a note that it was kept, the date it first entered, and the highest EPSS so far (the on-screen note says this too, in 10 languages).
- (0.1.8) **Changed: the weekly data update now lists, in the job summary, CVEs whose coverage shrank compared with last time.** Replaying the real data showed that SUSE's public data became unavailable for 2026 CVEs, which would silently shrink the SUSE and Leap coverage by 18 each for two KEV-based CVEs (a change on the source side that we cannot fix). The diff check was split out into a script with unit tests.
- **Command output in Markdown headings and tables** (0.1.9): an overview table, a path table, a table of fix points, details per number, the collection scope and the premises of the analysis, in that order. `--what-if-*` and `--baseline` are bullet lists too. The premises of the analysis are in the result, not on the standard error. Add `--plain` to return to the old prose form (check scripts that parse the output as text). `--help` now lists every option (10 languages), `--version` (`-V`) was added, and the order and names of the settings tabs match AccessScope and PersonalSOC.
- **Added: the values the LLM writes are checked against what it was given.** An IP address, domain, CVE ID or hash in the "Explain with LLM" text that is not in the summary it received is shown below the explanation under "Values not found in the collected material", as a possible invention. Only the strings are compared; the LLM is not asked. (0.2.0)
- **Added: the bundled known-vulnerability data grows from 53 to 64 entries.** Until now an entry was chosen because it is in the CISA KEV (exploited in real attacks) or because its FIRST EPSS score is high (likely to be exploited). A third basis is added: a CVE for which the Metasploit Framework has a public local privilege escalation module (11 entries; 50 from KEV, 3 EPSS only, 11 public module). It covers Ubuntu kernel flaws (overlayfs CVE-2023-2640 and CVE-2023-32629, and others), snapd, needrestart, Enlightenment and macOS (MacDirtyCow CVE-2022-46689, CVE-2024-27822). Entries from KEV and EPSS do not change. The CVE IDs come from a hand-picked list (`packaging/cve/public-exploit-cves.json`). No module code is included. The entry conditions are the same as for KEV and EPSS: local attack, high confidentiality and integrity impact, no high privilege required, and a fixed version from the vendor. (0.2.0)
- **Changed: entries added for a public module are not described as "exploited".** The reason text, the fix summary and the data note now separate three cases: exploited in real attacks, likely to be exploited, and a public exploit module exists. The third states that the module only exists and that exploitation is not confirmed (10 languages). (0.2.0)
- **Changed: CVEs that need a condition now carry it.** User namespaces for CVE-2022-34918, CVE-2023-2640 and CVE-2023-32629, and the SUID `enlightenment_sys` for CVE-2022-37706. A path is not reported where the condition is not met. (0.2.0)

## PersonalSOC

### 1.0.8

Keeps the investigation agent's hypotheses in a ledger.

- **Added: the investigation agent's hypotheses go into a ledger.** It keeps the outcome, the references to the evidence and the reason, and a withdrawn hypothesis does not disappear. Whether a cited reference exists is checked by code. The way an investigation ends is stated in a fixed sentence.
- **Added: `scan` output can be filtered with closed fields, no regular expressions** (`--filter`, `--filter-or`).
- **Added (off by default): a follow-up question, without tools, on a frozen investigation result** (`scan --llm X --followup "question"`).
- Verification: 387 workspace tests pass (without the GUI crate; Docker; 0 failures).

### 1.0.7

- **Fixed: Resource Guard kinds were shown as internal names and English values in other languages.** They now read as sentences in all 10 languages (for example "Crash loop: 3 restarts in 300s").
- Verified: The 365 tests pass (0 failures, 2 ignored as before).

### 1.0.6

- **Fixed: `personalsoc --version` printed the usage instead of the version and exited with code 2.** It now prints "personalsoc <version>" and exits with code 0.
- **Added: translations in 10 languages for 2 new FIM kinds from RoamSwitch.** They are "created during a package update and removed before it ended" and "changed during a package update and put back to its old content before it ended". Without them, the timeline summary would have shown the internal names.
- Checks: the 364 workspace tests pass, including 335 in the core (Mac; 0 failed; the 2 ignored were already there).

### 1.0.5

- **Fixed: settings RoamSwitch could not confirm were listed as "[Medium/Confirmed] Configuration weakness".** These are the items the health check marked [unconfirmed], for example when it did not run as root (Known Kernel CVE Exposure, exploited or likely-exploited vulnerabilities and the like). They are no longer counted as weaknesses; the overview box "RoamSwitch settings that could not be confirmed" shows their count and names. The overall risk score drops by that much. When an item that was a weakness last time could not be confirmed this time, it is counted as "cannot be compared" instead of "no longer seen (resolved)".
- **Fixed: RoamSwitch's "rebuilt the baseline for the log tampering comparison" record was ranked high as "[High/Likely] unresolved detection (T1685.006)".** It is a maintenance record, so it is no longer counted as an unresolved detection.
- **Fixed: IP addresses were written two ways in the same report** ("203[.]0[.]113[.]50" and "203.0.113.50"). Detection titles and descriptions now use "203.0.113.50". The IOC list keeps the defanged form as before.
- **Fixed: in PathScope detections, the sentence about the starting point ran into the one about fix points without a break** ("Paths start from user: admin 1 fix point"). They are now separated as "admin. 1 fix point" ("admin。遮断点は 1 か所" in Japanese). The note for zero paths also put a Japanese full stop into English; it is now one line per note.
- Checks: the 361 workspace tests pass, including 333 in the core (Mac; 0 failed; the 2 ignored were already there).

### 1.0.4

- **Fixed: `personalsoc … | head` printed a panic message.** It now exits with code 141 without it.
- **Fixed: the display language.** Detections that stopped appearing right after the language was changed, the description of detections from a Sensor on the same LAN (new Sensors), the Mac RoamSwitch timeline and eBPF guard records (without `[scope=…]`, as "action: blocked the traffic of service labapp.service" and the like) are shown in the display language. English, German, French, Spanish, Italian and Portuguese use the form that matches the number.
- **Fixed: techniques whose state is unknown were counted as stopped.** A technique whose state is unknown, such as right after RoamSwitch starts, is no longer counted as stopped and raises no "turn the guard on" detection; the overview shows "N techniques not known to be running". When an ordinary user cannot read RoamSwitch's quarantine, it is "cannot be confirmed" instead of "0".
- **Fixed: a broken snapshot was treated as the first run.** With a broken history snapshot (`snapshots.json`), "changes since the last check" becomes "cannot be confirmed (reason)". A broken `last.json` of the scheduled run sends one notification, and a broken `run.json` makes `schedule status` and the app say "cannot be confirmed". Broken files are kept as `.corrupt-…`, the newest 3. `history_unreadable` and `history_set_aside` are added to the JSON changes (only when unreadable).
- **Changed: the material given to the LLM is in English** (`scan --brief`, `analyze --brief`, the app's "summary input for the LLM", the field names of the report appendix JSON). The report is written in the display language, and the `--llm` draft moves from fixed Japanese to the display language. Secrets are masked as `[REDACTED]` in every language.
- Checks: the tests pass, including 328 in the core (Mac).

### 1.0.3

- **Fixed: an SSH attempt on a user that does not exist was counted twice for its two log lines.**
- **Fixed: the details of unresolved RoamSwitch detections are shown in the display language.**
- **Added: sentences in 10 languages are built from the sentence-kind code attached to RoamSwitch records.**
- **Changed: when run with sudo, the starting point of the PathScope paths is the user who ran sudo, and that starting point is shown in the output.**

### 1.0.2

- **Added: more is taken in from the RoamSwitch MCP.** Detection coverage, the SSH guard history, the Linux status tools, honeytokens, entropy detection (ransomware), and the supply-chain check. The investigation agent may use `search_exec_events` and `get_process_tree`, after the arguments are validated.
- **Added: the results of AccessScope, PathScope and Sensor are taken in with fixed arguments.** From Sensor it takes in, read-only, the LAN-layer threats that a Sensor on the same LAN sees (rogue DHCP servers and IPv6 routers, ARP spoofing, abnormal IoT behavior, weak unmanaged devices). When part of the range could not be read, it does not say "nothing found".
- **Added: an audit of AI agent settings** (Hooks, disabled permission prompts, Toxic Flow), **and more target clients for the MCP settings audit.**
- **Changed: the built-in log parsers were widened.** sshd-session (OpenSSH 9.8 and later), the size criterion for web 200 responses, eTLD+1, and auditd `EXECVE` and `PROCTITLE`.
- **Added: correlation rules** (they join detections from several sources), **history and a difference from the previous run** (`~/.local/state/personalsoc/history/snapshots.json`, 0600), `scan --format text|markdown|json|sarif`, `--fail-on`, and `--diff`.
- The translations in the 10 languages were reviewed (the Italian double negative, the Korean words for "detection" and "risk", the Chinese word for honeytoken, and others).
- Checks: the 316 workspace tests pass (on a Mac, without the window app). The Sensor intake was checked by connecting to a Sensor (a daemon of the new version) running on a real LAN; it read 16 devices and the threats.

### 0.1.0 - 1.0.1 (summarized)

The contents of these releases, grouped by theme. The full text of each entry is in this file's git history.

- (1.0.1) **Fixed: when the periodic check (`personalsoc scan --notify`) did not run, it ended silently.** When the settings cannot be read or collecting the logs fails, a notification with a fixed reason is shown besides the standard error (once a day; 10 languages). The reason and the day of notification are kept in `run.json` (`last_error`, `error_notified`) and cleared when a check succeeds. The time of the last success and the record of the sources that could not be read are kept during the failure. `schedule status` shows "The last check failed". When you have turned notifications off, a collection failure is only recorded (when the settings cannot be read it is not known whether they are off, so a notification is shown).
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: when the place to keep state (`HOME`) could not be decided, it ended silently as a success.** It now prints the reason to the standard error and exits with code 1.
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: failures to start the notification command (`osascript`, `notify-send`), and its exit code or signal, were discarded.** A sentence of facts is now written to the standard error (10 languages).
- (1.0.1) **Fixed: the sentence for a failed LLM showed `Some(1)` and an array notation (`[3, 5, 9]`).** The exit code is just a number, "terminated by signal N" for a signal, "terminated abnormally" when neither is known, and the evidence lines are written as "3, 5, 9". A following authentication hint no longer doubles the full stop.
- (1.0.1) **Added: the check of values written by the LLM now includes IPv6 addresses, e-mail addresses and destinations (`IP:port`).** The details are the same as in AccessScope 1.0.2.
- (1.0.1) **Changed: dependencies were updated** (rustls, rustls-webpki, tokio and others; advisories RUSTSEC-2026-0346 to 0349, 0382, 0383 and 0386; the `zip` advisory is removed with a `zip` 9 copy, as in AccessScope). The README now says that when used as root, the settings are read from `$HOME`.
- (1.0.1) Checks: 213 workspace tests pass, 1 fails and 1 is ignored. The failure predates this version: the app test `ipc_settings_are_localized...` fails because the Linux label "auditd" contains no kana.
- (1.0.0) **Changed: the version is now 1.0.0.** Sensor, PathScope and AccessScope move to 1.0.0 together with it.
- (1.0.0) **Fixed: a scheduled run said nothing about logs it could not read** (`personalsoc scan --notify`). A scheduled run works with ordinary user rights, so logs that only root can read (authentication, web server, auditd and others) cannot be read. It showed none of that, so you could not tell how much was covered. When some logs could not be read, it now notifies "N logs could not be read: names" on the first run and whenever the set of unreadable logs changes. When everything was readable, it notifies nothing. The details also go to standard error. To check those logs, run `sudo personalsoc scan`.
- (1.0.0) **Added: `personalsoc schedule status`.** It shows whether the scheduled run is registered, the time it last ran, and the logs it could not read. The settings window shows the same. The time of the last check and the unreadable logs are recorded in `run.json`.
- (1.0.0) **Fixed: on Linux you could not tell why a scheduled run showed no notification.** Without `notify-send` (package `libnotify-bin` on Debian and Ubuntu) no notification appears. The settings window and `schedule status` now say when it is missing, and when a notification cannot be shown it is written to standard error. A systemd user timer does not run while you are logged out; on a server, run `loginctl enable-linger` once. A missing linger is also reported in the settings window and `schedule status`. The README now says that a scheduled run uses ordinary user rights and mentions `sudo personalsoc scan` and `loginctl enable-linger`.
- (1.0.0) Verification: 194 workspace tests pass and 2 fail (1 ignored). Both failures predate this change: `timeout_kills_grandchildren` is a problem of the container environment, and the app test `ipc_settings_are_localized...` fails because the Linux "auditd" label contains no kana.
- **0.1.0**: first release of "PersonalSOC", a personal security operations center that gathers this device's logs and defense status, read-only, into a report. Findings, sources and IOCs are shown with charts; the UI has 10 languages and follows the OS language. "Check status" has your chosen LLM CLI (opencode, claude, codex, agy) investigate autonomously with read-only tools and write an audit report. LLM integration is optional and off by default. PersonalSOC itself has no network permission.
- **0.1.1**: in-app updater (Mac), which fetches version information only when you press the button and verifies the signature before installing. Removed the fixed "Response" section from reports and fixed chart legends and axis labels overlapping in English and other languages.
- **0.1.2**: reads more from RoamSwitch's MCP (package CVE matches, active vulnerability check status, what Sensor found from outside, protections switched off, honeytoken, browser-credential and ransomware detections, recovery readiness, preserved evidence, Air-Gap). Gatekeeper denials on Mac became an optional source (off by default). The LLM command's own tools are switched off at launch where possible (`claude` starts with built-in tools and MCP off; `agy` and `codex` cannot be locked, so they need the confirmation in Settings). Fixed the XProtect check matching process names and `codex` not running in an empty temporary folder.
- **0.1.3**: an LLM reply that contains raw line breaks or invalid escapes is repaired and parsed again. The Gatekeeper source is now labelled "check the record of denials", and the screen and README state that Gatekeeper behaves the same whether it is on or off (it only reads the record).
- **0.1.4**: secret masking now also covers quoted JSON and `Authorization`, `Bearer` and `Basic` headers. Log lines can no longer close the data fence handed to the LLM using the lookalike characters `<<<` and `>>>`. Scheduled runs (Mac) work in folders with spaces or non-ASCII characters, and an app opened straight from the DMG is told to move to the Applications folder (10 languages). When an LLM command times out, its child processes are terminated too. `opencode` is no longer treated as having its tools locked, because we have not confirmed the setting takes effect, so using it requires the confirmation in Settings (the README is updated too).
- **0.1.5**: Settings is split into tabs by function (Automatic status check, LLM integration, Updates, Display language); the Updates tab appears only where updating is supported (Mac). Removed the "it contains unverified text, so please check…" note from the message shown when a report has been created (10 languages).
- **0.1.6**: removed the margin around the app icon and made its background transparent. It used to come with a white margin. The rounded square now fills the whole icon on Mac, Linux and in the app window.
- **0.1.7**: on Linux, the drop-down lists (update interval, LLM choice, display language) had a white background, so their text could not be read in dark mode; fixed.
- **`--llm` with a file also shows the values the LLM wrote that are not in the summary it was given** (0.2.1): `personalsoc sshd <file> --llm name` and the like. The same list "Values not in the collected material" as in the report is shown right after the LLM's text.

---
- **0.1.8**: the details of a CVE (known vulnerability) finding now show the package name and version. The report table shows only the details, not the finding's title, so it was not clear which package a finding was about.
